He actually warned me I could be suspended if things don't improve. \n\nWhich is a shame, because the weekends are when we get the most foot traffic, especially right after church lets out. Everyone comes in looking for the Sunday paper and those little powdered donuts. We used to get our donuts from a local bakery, Mrs. Higgins'. She made the best crullers I've ever had in my life. Light as air. But she retired a few years ago and moved down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. My aunt lived in Sarasota for a while. She had this little condo right on the beach, and you could sit on the balcony and watch the pelicans dive for fish. Pelicans are fascinating birds, you know. They have those huge pouches under their beaks. I watched a documentary on PBS about them once. It was narrated by that British fellow, the one who does all the nature shows. He has a very soothing voice. I always fall asleep halfway through his programs. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at home it's... it's been an issue too. My wife, she's had to start covering commitments I used to handle. She said people were starting to get uncomfortable or confused around me when I talk. So she just does it all now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated when you notice your words jumping all over the place like that?\n\nPatient: Frustrated? Yeah... I guess I do get a bit frustrated sometimes. It's like my brain just wants to go in ten different directions at once. It reminds me of this old compass I had when I was a Boy Scout. \n\nWe used to go camping up in the state park, usually in the fall when the leaves were changing. The colors up there were just unbelievable—bright oranges and reds, like the whole forest was on fire. We'd hike for miles, and our scoutmaster, Mr. Henderson, he was a real stickler for orienteering. He made us use these heavy brass compasses and topographical maps. None of this GPS nonsense on your phone like kids have today. You actually had to know how to read the terrain and calculate the magnetic declination. I still have that compass somewhere, actually. It's probably in the attic, in that old cedar chest my grandfather built. He was a carpenter, made all his furniture by hand. Beautiful dovetail joints, no nails or screws. He built this incredible dining room table out of solid oak that weighed a ton. We had to hire three guys just to move it into the house when we inherited it. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, the words. Yeah, I don't know if frustrated is the right word, but I definitely notice it happening, and it's just... it's just how it is right now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to things that you're doing or listening to when you're not trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Paying attention? I mean, I think I do alright if I'm just listening to the radio or something. I usually have it on in the background when I'm driving. I drive a Honda CR-V, it's a 2014, but it runs like a top. I make sure to get the oil changed every 3,000 miles, religious about it. My mechanic, Sal, he runs this little shop over on Elm Street. He's been there for thirty years, honest guy. He always shows me the old parts when he replaces something, so you know he's not ripping you off. He showed me the air filter last time, and it was just caked in dust. We had that dry spell last month, you know, hardly any rain at all. My lawn completely browned out. I tried setting up the sprinklers, but the water pressure in our neighborhood has been terrible ever since they started building those new townhomes down the road. They're putting up fifty units on that old dairy farm property. It's a shame, really, seeing all that open space get paved over. \n\nBut, uh, paying attention... yeah, I haven't really noticed any big issues with that when I'm just doing my own thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble remembering things, like appointments or what you were saying, when your words start jumping around like that?\n\nPatient: Remembering things? No, I don't think my memory is too bad. I mean, I remember to take out the trash on Tuesdays. Tuesday is also when the recycling gets picked up, but they only take the blue bins every other week. It's a new system the city put in. They sent out these little calendars with stickers so you know which week is which. I put mine on the fridge, right next to this postcard my daughter sent me from her trip to Italy. She went to Rome and Florence. Said the pasta there was incredible, totally different from what we get here. They make it fresh every day, just flour and eggs. I tried making fresh pasta once. Bought one of those hand-crank machines at a garage sale. It clamped onto the counter, but I could never get the dough thin enough. It always came out chewy. The guy I bought it from, he was selling a whole bunch of kitchen stuff because he was moving into an RV to travel the country. Said he was going to visit all the national parks. Yosemite, Yellowstone, the Grand Canyon. I've always wanted to see the Grand Canyon. Just stand on the edge and look out at all those layers of rock. \n\nBut, um... memory? Yeah, no, I don't really have trouble remembering appointments or anything like that. I write them down on the calendar.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected and make sense to you, even if your words don't come out that way when you're talking to others?\n\nPatient: Connected? Yeah, in my head, they make perfect sense. It's just when I open my mouth, things get... complicated. That's actually why I came in. I'll be at work, trying to explain the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule to my team, and suddenly my words start jumping from the delivery trucks to Thursdays, and then to the church grill we used last summer, and then I'm talking about my boss's tone of voice. People just stare at me like they can't follow a word I'm saying. \n\nSpeaking of the church grill, we actually need to get a new one before the spring picnic. The old one rusted out completely on the bottom. It's one of those big barrel smokers, you know? We used to do brisket in it for twelve hours straight. My buddy Dave, he's the one who usually mans the grill, he uses this special rub with brown sugar and smoked paprika. He won't give anyone the exact recipe. Says it's a family secret passed down from his uncle in Texas. Texas is huge, though. I drove across it once in the nineties, took days just to get from El Paso to Houston. The highway just stretches on forever, flat as a pancake in some parts. We stopped at this little diner in the middle of nowhere and had the best chicken fried steak I've ever tasted in my life. The gravy was just... incredible. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... my thoughts. They make sense to me, but I can tell by the way people look at me at work that they aren't getting what I'm trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel embarrassed when others have trouble following what you're saying?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? Yeah, I guess so. It's frustrating, you know? I can see it in their eyes, that blank look. Like when you're trying to watch a movie and the cable cuts out right at the good part. We had that happen during the Super Bowl a few years back. The whole neighborhood lost power right in the third quarter. Everyone spilled out into the street, trying to figure out what was going on. Turns out a squirrel had chewed through a transformer down on 4th Avenue. Fried the poor thing instantly, and knocked out power for six blocks. We ended up dragging the grill out to the driveway—not the church grill, my own little charcoal one—and cooking all the hot dogs we had in the fridge before they went bad. My neighbor, Gary, he brought over some beers from his cooler. Gary works for the post office, walks like ten miles a day on his route. He goes through a pair of shoes every three months. He buys those special orthopedic ones with the thick soles because his arches are completely flat. I told him he should try inserts, but he says they just bunch up under his toes. \n\nBut, um... embarrassed. Yeah, I do feel embarrassed when they just stare at me and I know I've lost them. It makes me want to just stop talking altogether.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood or overall behavior since this word-jumping started happening?\n\nPatient: Changes in my mood or behavior? Well, I don't know about mood so much, but... my behavior at work has definitely gotten a little weird lately. Sometimes on the sales floor, I'll catch myself just pacing the same aisle, straightening the same row of batteries over and over again. Or I'll just be standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, staring at it, while the associates are all just standing around waiting for me to tell them what to do. \n\nThe batteries, they always get so messed up by the registers. People pick them up while they're waiting in line, look at the price, and then just shove them back on the wrong peg. It drives me crazy. We sell those big packs of AAs, the ones with like 36 in a box. You'd think people wouldn't need that many, but with all the kids' toys and remote controls these days, they go through them fast. I remember when I was a kid, the only thing that took batteries was my flashlight. We used those big, heavy D batteries. My dad kept a flashlight in the glove compartment of his old Ford truck. It was a '72 F-150, two-tone green and white. He loved that truck. He used to take me fishing in it every Sunday morning out at Miller's Pond. We'd catch bluegill mostly, sometimes a smallmouth bass if we were lucky. He had this old tackle box that smelled like rubber worms and rust. He taught me how to tie a clinch knot when I was seven. It's the only knot you really need for fishing, honestly. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, my behavior. Yeah, just... pacing the aisles, staring at the clipboard. Just standing there, really. It's like I get stuck in a loop or something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disorganized or hard to follow even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Disorganized when I'm just by myself? No, I don't think so. In my head, it all flows just fine when I'm not trying to push the words out of my mouth. Like, if I'm just driving home from the store, my mind is completely clear. \n\nI usually take the interstate, which is an absolute nightmare around six o'clock. They've been doing construction on the 4th Street overpass for... gosh, it has to be three years now. It's ridiculous. They shut down two lanes right before the exit, and you always get those people who try to zoom all the way to the front and merge at the last possible second. Drives me up the wall. I saw this guy in a cherry-red Corvette do it just yesterday. The car was spotless, though, I'll give him that. Looked like a classic, maybe an '84. \n\nMy brother-in-law, Steve, he bought an '84 Corvette right before he got divorced. Just drained his savings for it. His wife, Diane, she was absolutely furious. She packed up and moved down to Florida. Opened a dog grooming business in Tampa, I think. Or maybe Clearwater. The dogs down there must need a lot of grooming because of all the humidity, you know? The air just feels like a wet sponge. I went down there for a conference back in 2012, and the minute you step out of the airport, your shirt just sticks right to your back...\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty with explaining things and jumping between topics is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Affecting things... yeah, it really is. At home, my wife has had to start covering all the commitments I used to handle. She said people were just getting uncomfortable or confused around me, so she stepped in to take over. Like the neighborhood watch meetings. I used to run those. We meet at the community center on the second Tuesday of every month. The community center just got a new roof, actually. They used those architectural shingles, the ones that are supposed to look like slate but they're really just asphalt. They look nice, though, a dark charcoal color. The contractor who put them up is a guy named Mike. Mike's got this big yellow lab that rides in the back of his truck. Dog barks at every single red light. I don't know how Mike stands it, honestly, the noise right in his ear all day. I had a dog when I was a kid, a beagle named Buster. Beagles have that specific howl, you know? They don't just bark, they bay. You can hear a beagle from a mile away if they catch a rabbit's scent. We used to let him run in the woods behind our house before they tore them down to build the shopping mall. \n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, right, my wife and my friends. Yeah, she handles all that stuff now because I just confuse people. And it's not just at home, it's at the store, too. I’m not trusted to run the busy weekend shifts anymore. We had my performance review recently, and my boss straight up warned me that I could be suspended if things don’t improve. Which is... well, it's bad. Weekends are when we make all our numbers, especially on patio furniture this time of year. Those patio sets come in these massive boxes that take two people to lift, and the cardboard is always held together by those thick plastic straps that cut right into your hands if you don't wear gloves...\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are coherent and organized in your mind, but just not coming out that way when you speak?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. In my head, it's all lined up perfectly. I know exactly what I want to say, but then... it just comes out wrong. It's like my brain is moving faster than my mouth, or maybe my mouth is moving faster than my brain. \n\nIt reminds me of this old typewriter my grandfather had. It was an Underwood, one of those heavy black ones with the round glass keys. If you typed too fast, the little metal arms with the letters on them would all jam up together in the middle, and you'd have to reach in and untangle them. My fingers would always get covered in black ink from the ribbon. He used to buy those ribbons at a stationary store downtown, a place called Miller's Office Supply. The smell in that store was amazing—like fresh paper and pencil shavings and floor wax. Mr. Miller, he was this tiny man who always wore a bowtie, even on Saturdays. He had this massive cash register, brass, with the buttons you had to push down really hard, and it would make this loud *ding* when the drawer popped open. You don't hear that sound anymore. Now everything is just a beep. Beep, beep, beep all day at the store. The self-checkout lanes are the worst. \"Please place item in the bagging area.\" Over and over. I had a customer last week who got so mad at the machine she just left a whole cart of groceries sitting there, right in the middle of the aisle. Had a gallon of milk in there that just sat getting warm for an hour before anyone noticed...\n\nBut, um... yeah, my thoughts are fine. It's just getting them out that's the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Focusing on one task? No, not really. If I'm just working on something by myself, I can stick with it. Like doing the inventory logs at work, I can sit there and count stock for hours without a problem. \n\nInventory is actually my favorite part of the job. You get to go into the back room where it's quiet, away from the customers. We have these massive metal shelves, the industrial kind that are bolted to the floor. They had to bring in a special crew just to install them back in 2018. The crew was from Ohio, I think. They drove this huge white van with no logo on the side, just a plain white van. Always makes me think of those spy movies, you know? The ones where they're sitting in the back with all the surveillance equipment, listening in on people's conversations through parabolic microphones. I saw a documentary about that once, how they can point a microphone at a window and pick up the vibrations on the glass to hear what you're saying inside. Glass is fascinating like that. It's technically a liquid, they say, just moving really, really slowly. That's why the windows in old churches are thicker at the bottom. We went to this cathedral in Europe on our honeymoon, my wife and I. The stained glass was incredible, all these deep blues and reds... \n\nBut, uh, no, focusing isn't an issue for me. It's really just the talking part that gets messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble reading or understanding written text, such as instructions or documents?\n\nPatient: Reading? No, reading is fine. I don't have any trouble with that. I read the instruction manuals for the new displays all the time. \n\nActually, they just sent us the fall promotional materials last week. The graphics are really something this year, all these vibrant oranges and deep browns. They print them on this high-gloss paper that feels really smooth when you run your hand over it. It smells like fresh ink when you first open the box, kind of chemical but also sweet, you know? It reminds me of the darkroom we had in college. I took a photography elective my sophomore year. We had to roll our own film onto these little plastic spools in pitch black. If you messed up, the whole roll was ruined. I ruined a lot of rolls before I got the hang of it. We were shooting black and white mostly, using this developer fluid that stained your fingers brown for days. My professor was this guy named Aris, he always wore these thick, round glasses and a corduroy jacket with elbow patches. Real classic academic look. He smoked a pipe, too, which you couldn't even do on campus anymore, but he did it anyway right outside the arts building. Cherry tobacco, I think. Smelled like the holidays. My uncle used to smoke cherry tobacco. He lived up in Maine, had this cabin right on a lake...\n\nBut, um... no, I can read just fine. Documents and instructions are no problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with explaining things and jumping between topics has always been a part of how you communicate, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't always been like this. He started really pushing us hard on the forecasts and the weekly schedules, wanting everything projected out a month in advance. \n\nHe's this younger guy, straight out of some corporate training program, always wearing these really sharp, tailored suits. Navy blue, usually, with a pale pink shirt. You don't see a lot of guys wearing pink shirts anymore, but it works for him. He drives this immaculate silver Lexus, parks it right in the fire lane when he visits the store. Just leaves it running sometimes. I always wonder what kind of gas mileage he gets, leaving it idling like that. I had a Toyota back in the nineties that got terrible mileage. It was this faded red color, the clear coat was peeling off the hood so it looked like it had a sunburn. We drove that car all the way to Yellowstone one summer. The kids were small then, fighting in the backseat the whole way across South Dakota. Wall Drug, that's where we stopped. They have these signs for hundreds of miles... \"Free Ice Water at Wall Drug.\" We bought a jackalope postcard there. My daughter thought it was a real animal, a rabbit with antlers. She kept looking out the window for one the rest of the trip...\n\nBut, yeah, it hasn't been a lifelong thing. Just the last 11 months or so, since the new manager started pressing us on all the scheduling stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder, or do you think you might have some symptoms of it?\n\nPatient: ADHD? No, I've never been diagnosed with anything like that. I don't think I have those symptoms, either. I mean, my kids used to bounce off the walls when they were little, but me? No. \n\nThough my grandson, he's got all that energy now. He's seven, just turned seven in October. We got him this big trampoline for his birthday, one of those ones with the safety net all the way around so they don't bounce off into the bushes. Setting that thing up was a nightmare. The springs are so tight, you have to use this special metal hook tool to pull them into place, and if your hand slips, it snaps back and takes a chunk out of your knuckle. I was out there in the backyard for three hours wrestling with it. The grass was still wet from the morning dew, so my knees were completely soaked by the time I finished. It's funny how dew works, just condensation from the temperature dropping overnight. It always reminds me of camping out in the backyard when I was a kid. We had this old canvas tent that smelled like mildew, and we'd stay up late telling ghost stories with a flashlight under our chins. We used to catch fireflies in a mason jar and poke holes in the lid so they could breathe...\n\nBut, uh, no. No ADHD for me. Never had a diagnosis for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I told him he should try inserts, but he says they just bunch up under his toes. \n\nBut, um... embarrassed. Yeah, I do feel embarrassed when they just stare at me and I know I've lost them. Well, I don't know about mood so much, but... my behavior at work has definitely gotten a little weird lately. Sometimes on the sales floor, I'll catch myself just pacing the same aisle, straightening the same row of batteries over and over again. Or I'll just be standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, staring at it, while the associates are all just standing around waiting for me to tell them what to do. \n\nThe batteries, they always get so messed up by the registers. People pick them up while they're waiting in line, look at the price, and then just shove them back on the wrong peg. It drives me crazy. We sell those big packs of AAs, the ones with like 36 in a box. You'd think people wouldn't need that many, but with all the kids' toys and remote controls these days, they go through them fast. I remember when I was a kid, the only thing that took batteries was my flashlight. We used those big, heavy D batteries. My dad kept a flashlight in the glove compartment of his old Ford truck. It was a '72 F-150, two-tone green and white. He loved that truck. He used to take me fishing in it every Sunday morning out at Miller's Pond. We'd catch bluegill mostly, sometimes a smallmouth bass if we were lucky. He had this old tackle box that smelled like rubber worms and rust. He taught me how to tie a clinch knot when I was seven. It's the only knot you really need for fishing, honestly. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, my behavior. Yeah, just... pacing the aisles, staring at the clipboard. Just standing there, really. It's like I get stuck in a loop or something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disorganized or hard to follow even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Disorganized when I'm just by myself? No, I don't think so. In my head, it all flows just fine when I'm not trying to push the words out of my mouth. Like, if I'm just driving home from the store, my mind is completely clear. \n\nI usually take the interstate, which is an absolute nightmare around six o'clock. They've been doing construction on the 4th Street overpass for... gosh, it has to be three years now. It's ridiculous. They shut down two lanes right before the exit, and you always get those people who try to zoom all the way to the front and merge at the last possible second. Drives me up the wall. I saw this guy in a cherry-red Corvette do it just yesterday. The car was spotless, though, I'll give him that. Looked like a classic, maybe an '84. \n\nMy brother-in-law, Steve, he bought an '84 Corvette right before he got divorced. Just drained his savings for it. His wife, Diane, she was absolutely furious. She packed up and moved down to Florida. Opened a dog grooming business in Tampa, I think. Or maybe Clearwater. The dogs down there must need a lot of grooming because of all the humidity, you know? The air just feels like a wet sponge. I went down there for a conference back in 2012, and the minute you step out of the airport, your shirt just sticks right to your back...\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty with explaining things and jumping between topics is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Affecting things... yeah, it really is. At home, my wife has had to start covering all the commitments I used to handle. She said people were just getting uncomfortable or confused around me, so she stepped in to take over. Like the neighborhood watch meetings. I used to run those. We meet at the community center on the second Tuesday of every month. The community center just got a new roof, actually. They used those architectural shingles, the ones that are supposed to look like slate but they're really just asphalt. They look nice, though, a dark charcoal color. The contractor who put them up is a guy named Mike. Mike's got this big yellow lab that rides in the back of his truck. Dog barks at every single red light. I don't know how Mike stands it, honestly, the noise right in his ear all day. I had a dog when I was a kid, a beagle named Buster. Beagles have that specific howl, you know? They don't just bark, they bay. You can hear a beagle from a mile away if they catch a rabbit's scent. We used to let him run in the woods behind our house before they tore them down to build the shopping mall. \n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, right, my wife and my friends. Yeah, she handles all that stuff now because I just confuse people. And it's not just at home, it's at the store, too. I’m not trusted to run the busy weekend shifts anymore. We had my performance review recently, and my boss straight up warned me that I could be suspended if things don’t improve. Which is... well, it's bad. Weekends are when we make all our numbers, especially on patio furniture this time of year. Those patio sets come in these massive boxes that take two people to lift, and the cardboard is always held together by those thick plastic straps that cut right into your hands if you don't wear gloves...\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are coherent and organized in your mind, but just not coming out that way when you speak?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. In my head, it's all lined up perfectly. I know exactly what I want to say, but then... it just comes out wrong. It's like my brain is moving faster than my mouth, or maybe my mouth is moving faster than my brain. \n\nIt reminds me of this old typewriter my grandfather had. It was an Underwood, one of those heavy black ones with the round glass keys. If you typed too fast, the little metal arms with the letters on them would all jam up together in the middle, and you'd have to reach in and untangle them. My fingers would always get covered in black ink from the ribbon. He used to buy those ribbons at a stationary store downtown, a place called Miller's Office Supply. The smell in that store was amazing—like fresh paper and pencil shavings and floor wax. Mr. Miller, he was this tiny man who always wore a bowtie, even on Saturdays. He had this massive cash register, brass, with the buttons you had to push down really hard, and it would make this loud *ding* when the drawer popped open. You don't hear that sound anymore. Now everything is just a beep. Beep, beep, beep all day at the store. The self-checkout lanes are the worst. \"Please place item in the bagging area.\" Over and over. I had a customer last week who got so mad at the machine she just left a whole cart of groceries sitting there, right in the middle of the aisle. Had a gallon of milk in there that just sat getting warm for an hour before anyone noticed...\n\nBut, um... yeah, my thoughts are fine. It's just getting them out that's the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Focusing on one task? No, not really. If I'm just working on something by myself, I can stick with it. Like doing the inventory logs at work, I can sit there and count stock for hours without a problem. \n\nInventory is actually my favorite part of the job. You get to go into the back room where it's quiet, away from the customers. We have these massive metal shelves, the industrial kind that are bolted to the floor. They had to bring in a special crew just to install them back in 2018. The crew was from Ohio, I think. They drove this huge white van with no logo on the side, just a plain white van. Always makes me think of those spy movies, you know? The ones where they're sitting in the back with all the surveillance equipment, listening in on people's conversations through parabolic microphones. I saw a documentary about that once, how they can point a microphone at a window and pick up the vibrations on the glass to hear what you're saying inside. Glass is fascinating like that. It's technically a liquid, they say, just moving really, really slowly. That's why the windows in old churches are thicker at the bottom. We went to this cathedral in Europe on our honeymoon, my wife and I. The stained glass was incredible, all these deep blues and reds... \n\nBut, uh, no, focusing isn't an issue for me. It's really just the talking part that gets messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble reading or understanding written text, such as instructions or documents?\n\nPatient: Reading? No, reading is fine. I don't have any trouble with that. I read the instruction manuals for the new displays all the time. \n\nActually, they just sent us the fall promotional materials last week. The graphics are really something this year, all these vibrant oranges and deep browns. They print them on this high-gloss paper that feels really smooth when you run your hand over it. It smells like fresh ink when you first open the box, kind of chemical but also sweet, you know? It reminds me of the darkroom we had in college. I took a photography elective my sophomore year. We had to roll our own film onto these little plastic spools in pitch black. If you messed up, the whole roll was ruined. I ruined a lot of rolls before I got the hang of it. We were shooting black and white mostly, using this developer fluid that stained your fingers brown for days. My professor was this guy named Aris, he always wore these thick, round glasses and a corduroy jacket with elbow patches. Real classic academic look. He smoked a pipe, too, which you couldn't even do on campus anymore, but he did it anyway right outside the arts building. Cherry tobacco, I think. Smelled like the holidays. My uncle used to smoke cherry tobacco. He lived up in Maine, had this cabin right on a lake...\n\nBut, um... no, I can read just fine. Documents and instructions are no problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with explaining things and jumping between topics has always been a part of how you communicate, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't always been like this. It's really been... I don't know, maybe building up for about 11 months now? Yeah, right around the time the new district manager took over. He started really pushing us hard on the forecasts and the weekly schedules, wanting everything projected out a month in advance. \n\nHe's this younger guy, straight out of some corporate training program, always wearing these really sharp, tailored suits. Navy blue, usually, with a pale pink shirt. You don't see a lot of guys wearing pink shirts anymore, but it works for him. He drives this immaculate silver Lexus, parks it right in the fire lane when he visits the store. Just leaves it running sometimes. I always wonder what kind of gas mileage he gets, leaving it idling like that. I had a Toyota back in the nineties that got terrible mileage. It was this faded red color, the clear coat was peeling off the hood so it looked like it had a sunburn. We drove that car all the way to Yellowstone one summer. The kids were small then, fighting in the backseat the whole way across South Dakota. Wall Drug, that's where we stopped. They have these signs for hundreds of miles... \"Free Ice Water at Wall Drug.\" We bought a jackalope postcard there. My daughter thought it was a real animal, a rabbit with antlers. She kept looking out the window for one the rest of the trip...\n\nBut, yeah, it hasn't been a lifelong thing. Just the last 11 months or so, since the new manager started pressing us on all the scheduling stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder, or do you think you might have some symptoms of it?\n\nPatient: ADHD? No, I've never been diagnosed with anything like that. I don't think I have those symptoms, either. I mean, my kids used to bounce off the walls when they were little, but me? No. \n\nThough my grandson, he's got all that energy now. He's seven, just turned seven in October. We got him this big trampoline for his birthday, one of those ones with the safety net all the way around so they don't bounce off into the bushes. Setting that thing up was a nightmare. The springs are so tight, you have to use this special metal hook tool to pull them into place, and if your hand slips, it snaps back and takes a chunk out of your knuckle. I was out there in the backyard for three hours wrestling with it. The grass was still wet from the morning dew, so my knees were completely soaked by the time I finished. It's funny how dew works, just condensation from the temperature dropping overnight. It always reminds me of camping out in the backyard when I was a kid. We had this old canvas tent that smelled like mildew, and we'd stay up late telling ghost stories with a flashlight under our chins. We used to catch fireflies in a mason jar and poke holes in the lid so they could breathe...\n\nBut, uh, no. No ADHD for me. Never had a diagnosis for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. School? No, I didn't have any trouble like that. I did fine, got decent grades. I was a pretty quiet kid, mostly just kept to myself and did my work. 

I actually really liked getting ready for school every year, you know, buying the supplies in September. I always had to have one of those Trapper Keepers. The velcro made this loud tearing sound every time you opened it in a quiet classroom. Mine had a picture of a space shuttle on the front. Columbia, I think it was. I used to watch the shuttle launches on this little box TV my dad kept out in the garage. He was always out there tinkering, fixing lawnmowers and radios for the neighbors. He had this giant pegboard wall where every single tool was outlined in black marker, so you knew exactly where it belonged when you were done with it. I actually tried to implement a system like that in the stockroom at my store, drawing outlines for the pricing guns and the box cutters. But the new kids they hire now, they just toss things wherever. Those pricing guns are not cheap, either. If you drop them on the concrete floor, the ink rollers get completely jammed up, and you get this black ink all over your hands that takes two days of scrubbing with pumice soap to get off. Pumice soap has that gritty texture, kind of like wet sand at the beach...

But, um... no. School was fine. No problems with learning or behavior or anything.

I usually help with the hotdogs, but the propane starter on that grill has been completely shot since last month, so we've been having to use those long matches to get it going, which I don't like doing. But anyway, I'll go from the trucks to Thursdays to the church grill, and then to my boss’s tone of voice. He has this real sharp tone lately, sounds just like my old high school baseball coach. \n\nAnd my team... they just stand there and look at me like they absolutely cannot follow what I’m saying. They just stare. So, I guess that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Does this jumping between topics with your words happen only at work when you're explaining things, or does it also happen when you're talking to friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it's not just at work, no. I mean, I was on the phone with my sister the other day, and she asked me about the same thing, actually. She lives out in Ohio now. Moved out there about ten years ago when her husband got transferred with the postal service. He’s a route manager or something now. They get a lot of snow out there, which I could never deal with. I hate shoveling snow. It always tweaks my lower back, right where I pulled a muscle helping my buddy move a couch back in the nineties. It was this ugly floral pattern couch, weighed a ton. But yeah, she said I was hard to follow on the phone, too. So I suppose it's happening other places, yeah.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when this word-jumping happens, especially when you're trying to explain things to others?\n\nPatient: Anxious? I don't really know if I'd call it anxious. I haven't really noticed feeling stressed out when the words do that, honestly. I just want them to understand the schedule and get the work done. \n\nBut if you want to talk about stress, the real stress is the new inventory software corporate rolled out last quarter. It's supposed to be fully automated, but the handheld scanners keep disconnecting from the Wi-Fi. We have this one router in the back breakroom, right next to the microwave. And every time someone heats up their lunch—like Jimmy, one of the stock boys, he always brings these frozen lasagna meals. The ones with the extra cheese. They smell great, actually, but they take a full five minutes to heat up in there. And for those five minutes, the Wi-Fi just completely drops out on the floor. \n\nI read somewhere that microwaves run on the exact same frequency as wireless routers. I think it was in a magazine at the dentist's office. Dr. Henderson, he's been my dentist for twenty years now. Good guy, but he always tells me I need to floss more. I try, but my gums are just too sensitive for it. I bought that special tape floss, but it still catches on this one crown I have in the back. \n\nSo... wait, what were you asking me just now? Something about the floor workers?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble understanding what others are saying when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: Trouble understanding? No, I don't think so. I hear what people are saying just fine. Like yesterday, one of the cashiers, Sarah, she was asking me about getting her shift covered this weekend. She's going to some concert downtown. I think it's a country music thing. \n\nI used to listen to a lot of country back in college, mostly Willie Nelson and Johnny Cash. My dad had this old record player out in the garage, and we'd listen to it while we worked on his '68 Chevy Impala. Beautiful car. It was cherry red, with the white leather interior. You just don't see cars made like that anymore, you know? Everything now is just plastic and computers. If a part breaks, you can't even fix it yourself with a wrench, you have to take it to the dealership and they charge you an arm and a leg just to plug a little diagnostic machine into the dashboard. It's a real racket they've got going on. My neighbor just paid a thousand dollars for a sensor. \n\nBut yeah, no, when people talk to me, I understand the words they say perfectly fine. It's just my own words that get all jumbled up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what you're doing or saying when you're not explaining something or talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Focus on what I'm doing? I don't know, I guess I haven't really noticed any trouble with that. If I'm just working by myself, like doing the inventory counts in the back, I just do it. \n\nI usually like doing the inventory, actually. It's quiet back there, away from the registers. We have these big metal racks, the industrial kind, you know? They're supposed to hold up to a thousand pounds each. We got them from this liquidator down in the industrial park when a warehouse went out of business a few years back. My boss rented a U-Haul and we spent the whole weekend moving them. It rained the entire time, just pouring buckets. We had to put tarps over everything, and my boots got completely soaked through. I had to throw them out after that. They were good boots, too. Red Wings. I had them for five years, broke them in perfectly. You can't just replace a pair of boots like that, it takes months to get the leather to mold to your feet right. I tried buying a new pair, but they gave me terrible blisters on my heels. \n\nSo... wait, focus on what? No, I think I focus okay when I'm just doing my own thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you having any problems with your daily activities because of this word-jumping issue?\n\nPatient: Problems with daily activities? Well, I mean, I guess things have changed a bit at work. My boss... he sat me down after my performance review and said I'm not trusted to run the busy weekend shifts anymore. He actually warned me I could be suspended if things don't improve. \n\nWhich is a shame, because the weekends are when we get the most foot traffic, especially right after church lets out. Everyone comes in looking for the Sunday paper and those little powdered donuts. We used to get our donuts from a local bakery, Mrs. Higgins'. She made the best crullers I've ever had in my life. Light as air. But she retired a few years ago and moved down to Florida. Naples, I think. Or maybe Sarasota. My aunt lived in Sarasota for a while. She had this little condo right on the beach, and you could sit on the balcony and watch the pelicans dive for fish. Pelicans are fascinating birds, you know. They have those huge pouches under their beaks. I watched a documentary on PBS about them once. It was narrated by that British fellow, the one who does all the nature shows. He has a very soothing voice. I always fall asleep halfway through his programs. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at home it's... it's been an issue too. My wife, she's had to start covering commitments I used to handle. She said people were starting to get uncomfortable or confused around me when I talk. So she just does it all now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated when you notice your words jumping all over the place like that?\n\nPatient: Frustrated? Yeah... I guess I do get a bit frustrated sometimes. It's like my brain just wants to go in ten different directions at once. It reminds me of this old compass I had when I was a Boy Scout. \n\nWe used to go camping up in the state park, usually in the fall when the leaves were changing. The colors up there were just unbelievable—bright oranges and reds, like the whole forest was on fire. We'd hike for miles, and our scoutmaster, Mr. Henderson, he was a real stickler for orienteering. He made us use these heavy brass compasses and topographical maps. None of this GPS nonsense on your phone like kids have today. You actually had to know how to read the terrain and calculate the magnetic declination. I still have that compass somewhere, actually. It's probably in the attic, in that old cedar chest my grandfather built. He was a carpenter, made all his furniture by hand. Beautiful dovetail joints, no nails or screws. He built this incredible dining room table out of solid oak that weighed a ton. We had to hire three guys just to move it into the house when we inherited it. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, the words. Yeah, I don't know if frustrated is the right word, but I definitely notice it happening, and it's just... it's just how it is right now, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention to things that you're doing or listening to when you're not trying to explain something to someone?\n\nPatient: Paying attention? I mean, I think I do alright if I'm just listening to the radio or something. I usually have it on in the background when I'm driving. I drive a Honda CR-V, it's a 2014, but it runs like a top. I make sure to get the oil changed every 3,000 miles, religious about it. My mechanic, Sal, he runs this little shop over on Elm Street. He's been there for thirty years, honest guy. He always shows me the old parts when he replaces something, so you know he's not ripping you off. He showed me the air filter last time, and it was just caked in dust. We had that dry spell last month, you know, hardly any rain at all. My lawn completely browned out. I tried setting up the sprinklers, but the water pressure in our neighborhood has been terrible ever since they started building those new townhomes down the road. They're putting up fifty units on that old dairy farm property. It's a shame, really, seeing all that open space get paved over. \n\nBut, uh, paying attention... yeah, I haven't really noticed any big issues with that when I'm just doing my own thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble remembering things, like appointments or what you were saying, when your words start jumping around like that?\n\nPatient: Remembering things? No, I don't think my memory is too bad. I mean, I remember to take out the trash on Tuesdays. Tuesday is also when the recycling gets picked up, but they only take the blue bins every other week. It's a new system the city put in. They sent out these little calendars with stickers so you know which week is which. I put mine on the fridge, right next to this postcard my daughter sent me from her trip to Italy. She went to Rome and Florence. Said the pasta there was incredible, totally different from what we get here. They make it fresh every day, just flour and eggs. I tried making fresh pasta once. Bought one of those hand-crank machines at a garage sale. It clamped onto the counter, but I could never get the dough thin enough. It always came out chewy. The guy I bought it from, he was selling a whole bunch of kitchen stuff because he was moving into an RV to travel the country. Said he was going to visit all the national parks. Yosemite, Yellowstone, the Grand Canyon. I've always wanted to see the Grand Canyon. Just stand on the edge and look out at all those layers of rock. \n\nBut, um... memory? Yeah, no, I don't really have trouble remembering appointments or anything like that. I write them down on the calendar.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected and make sense to you, even if your words don't come out that way when you're talking to others?\n\nPatient: Connected? Yeah, in my head, they make perfect sense. It's just when I open my mouth, things get... complicated. That's actually why I came in. I'll be at work, trying to explain the inventory numbers or the weekend schedule to my team, and suddenly my words start jumping from the delivery trucks to Thursdays, and then to the church grill we used last summer, and then I'm talking about my boss's tone of voice. People just stare at me like they can't follow a word I'm saying. \n\nSpeaking of the church grill, we actually need to get a new one before the spring picnic. The old one rusted out completely on the bottom. It's one of those big barrel smokers, you know? We used to do brisket in it for twelve hours straight. My buddy Dave, he's the one who usually mans the grill, he uses this special rub with brown sugar and smoked paprika. He won't give anyone the exact recipe. Says it's a family secret passed down from his uncle in Texas. Texas is huge, though. I drove across it once in the nineties, took days just to get from El Paso to Houston. The highway just stretches on forever, flat as a pancake in some parts. We stopped at this little diner in the middle of nowhere and had the best chicken fried steak I've ever tasted in my life. The gravy was just... incredible. \n\nBut, uh, yeah... my thoughts. They make sense to me, but I can tell by the way people look at me at work that they aren't getting what I'm trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel embarrassed when others have trouble following what you're saying?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? Yeah, I guess so. It's frustrating, you know? I can see it in their eyes, that blank look. Like when you're trying to watch a movie and the cable cuts out right at the good part. We had that happen during the Super Bowl a few years back. The whole neighborhood lost power right in the third quarter. Everyone spilled out into the street, trying to figure out what was going on. Turns out a squirrel had chewed through a transformer down on 4th Avenue. Fried the poor thing instantly, and knocked out power for six blocks. We ended up dragging the grill out to the driveway—not the church grill, my own little charcoal one—and cooking all the hot dogs we had in the fridge before they went bad. My neighbor, Gary, he brought over some beers from his cooler. Gary works for the post office, walks like ten miles a day on his route. He goes through a pair of shoes every three months. He buys those special orthopedic ones with the thick soles because his arches are completely flat. I told him he should try inserts, but he says they just bunch up under his toes. \n\nBut, um... embarrassed. Yeah, I do feel embarrassed when they just stare at me and I know I've lost them. It makes me want to just stop talking altogether.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood or overall behavior since this word-jumping started happening?\n\nPatient: Changes in my mood or behavior? Well, I don't know about mood so much, but... my behavior at work has definitely gotten a little weird lately. Sometimes on the sales floor, I'll catch myself just pacing the same aisle, straightening the same row of batteries over and over again. Or I'll just be standing there with the schedule clipboard in my hand, staring at it, while the associates are all just standing around waiting for me to tell them what to do. \n\nThe batteries, they always get so messed up by the registers. People pick them up while they're waiting in line, look at the price, and then just shove them back on the wrong peg. It drives me crazy. We sell those big packs of AAs, the ones with like 36 in a box. You'd think people wouldn't need that many, but with all the kids' toys and remote controls these days, they go through them fast. I remember when I was a kid, the only thing that took batteries was my flashlight. We used those big, heavy D batteries. My dad kept a flashlight in the glove compartment of his old Ford truck. It was a '72 F-150, two-tone green and white. He loved that truck. He used to take me fishing in it every Sunday morning out at Miller's Pond. We'd catch bluegill mostly, sometimes a smallmouth bass if we were lucky. He had this old tackle box that smelled like rubber worms and rust. He taught me how to tie a clinch knot when I was seven. It's the only knot you really need for fishing, honestly. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Oh, right, my behavior. Yeah, just... pacing the aisles, staring at the clipboard. Just standing there, really. It's like I get stuck in a loop or something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disorganized or hard to follow even when you're not trying to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Disorganized when I'm just by myself? No, I don't think so. In my head, it all flows just fine when I'm not trying to push the words out of my mouth. Like, if I'm just driving home from the store, my mind is completely clear. \n\nI usually take the interstate, which is an absolute nightmare around six o'clock. They've been doing construction on the 4th Street overpass for... gosh, it has to be three years now. It's ridiculous. They shut down two lanes right before the exit, and you always get those people who try to zoom all the way to the front and merge at the last possible second. Drives me up the wall. I saw this guy in a cherry-red Corvette do it just yesterday. The car was spotless, though, I'll give him that. I went down there for a conference back in 2012, and the minute you step out of the airport, your shirt just sticks right to your back...\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulty with explaining things and jumping between topics is affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Affecting things... yeah, it really is. At home, my wife has had to start covering all the commitments I used to handle. She said people were just getting uncomfortable or confused around me, so she stepped in to take over. Like the neighborhood watch meetings. I used to run those. We meet at the community center on the second Tuesday of every month. The community center just got a new roof, actually. They used those architectural shingles, the ones that are supposed to look like slate but they're really just asphalt. They look nice, though, a dark charcoal color. The contractor who put them up is a guy named Mike. Mike's got this big yellow lab that rides in the back of his truck. Dog barks at every single red light. I don't know how Mike stands it, honestly, the noise right in his ear all day. I had a dog when I was a kid, a beagle named Buster. Beagles have that specific howl, you know? They don't just bark, they bay. You can hear a beagle from a mile away if they catch a rabbit's scent. We used to let him run in the woods behind our house before they tore them down to build the shopping mall. \n\nBut, um, what was the question? Oh, right, my wife and my friends. Those patio sets come in these massive boxes that take two people to lift, and the cardboard is always held together by those thick plastic straps that cut right into your hands if you don't wear gloves...\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are coherent and organized in your mind, but just not coming out that way when you speak?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. In my head, it's all lined up perfectly. I know exactly what I want to say, but then... it just comes out wrong. It's like my brain is moving faster than my mouth, or maybe my mouth is moving faster than my brain. \n\nIt reminds me of this old typewriter my grandfather had. It was an Underwood, one of those heavy black ones with the round glass keys. If you typed too fast, the little metal arms with the letters on them would all jam up together in the middle, and you'd have to reach in and untangle them. My fingers would always get covered in black ink from the ribbon. He used to buy those ribbons at a stationary store downtown, a place called Miller's Office Supply. The smell in that store was amazing—like fresh paper and pencil shavings and floor wax. Mr. Miller, he was this tiny man who always wore a bowtie, even on Saturdays. He had this massive cash register, brass, with the buttons you had to push down really hard, and it would make this loud *ding* when the drawer popped open. You don't hear that sound anymore. Now everything is just a beep. Beep, beep, beep all day at the store. The self-checkout lanes are the worst. \"Please place item in the bagging area.\" Over and over. I had a customer last week who got so mad at the machine she just left a whole cart of groceries sitting there, right in the middle of the aisle. Had a gallon of milk in there that just sat getting warm for an hour before anyone noticed...\n\nBut, um... yeah, my thoughts are fine. It's just getting them out that's the problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time when you're not talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Focusing on one task? No, not really. If I'm just working on something by myself, I can stick with it. Like doing the inventory logs at work, I can sit there and count stock for hours without a problem. \n\nInventory is actually my favorite part of the job. You get to go into the back room where it's quiet, away from the customers. We have these massive metal shelves, the industrial kind that are bolted to the floor. They had to bring in a special crew just to install them back in 2018. The crew was from Ohio, I think. They drove this huge white van with no logo on the side, just a plain white van. Always makes me think of those spy movies, you know? The ones where they're sitting in the back with all the surveillance equipment, listening in on people's conversations through parabolic microphones. I saw a documentary about that once, how they can point a microphone at a window and pick up the vibrations on the glass to hear what you're saying inside. Glass is fascinating like that. It's technically a liquid, they say, just moving really, really slowly. That's why the windows in old churches are thicker at the bottom. We went to this cathedral in Europe on our honeymoon, my wife and I. The stained glass was incredible, all these deep blues and reds... \n\nBut, uh, no, focusing isn't an issue for me. It's really just the talking part that gets messed up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble reading or understanding written text, such as instructions or documents?\n\nPatient: Reading? No, reading is fine. I don't have any trouble with that. I read the instruction manuals for the new displays all the time. \n\nActually, they just sent us the fall promotional materials last week. The graphics are really something this year, all these vibrant oranges and deep browns. They print them on this high-gloss paper that feels really smooth when you run your hand over it. It smells like fresh ink when you first open the box, kind of chemical but also sweet, you know? It reminds me of the darkroom we had in college. I took a photography elective my sophomore year. We had to roll our own film onto these little plastic spools in pitch black. If you messed up, the whole roll was ruined. I ruined a lot of rolls before I got the hang of it. We were shooting black and white mostly, using this developer fluid that stained your fingers brown for days. He smoked a pipe, too, which you couldn't even do on campus anymore, but he did it anyway right outside the arts building. Cherry tobacco, I think. Smelled like the holidays. Documents and instructions are no problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with explaining things and jumping between topics has always been a part of how you communicate, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't always been like this. He started really pushing us hard on the forecasts and the weekly schedules, wanting everything projected out a month in advance. \n\nHe's this younger guy, straight out of some corporate training program, always wearing these really sharp, tailored suits. I always wonder what kind of gas mileage he gets, leaving it idling like that. I had a Toyota back in the nineties that got terrible mileage. The kids were small then, fighting in the backseat the whole way across South Dakota. Wall Drug, that's where we stopped. Just the last 11 months or so, since the new manager started pressing us on all the scheduling stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder, or do you think you might have some symptoms of it?\n\nPatient: ADHD? No, I've never been diagnosed with anything like that. I don't think I have those symptoms, either. I mean, my kids used to bounce off the walls when they were little, but me? No. \n\nThough my grandson, he's got all that energy now. He's seven, just turned seven in October. We got him this big trampoline for his birthday, one of those ones with the safety net all the way around so they don't bounce off into the bushes. Setting that thing up was a nightmare. The springs are so tight, you have to use this special metal hook tool to pull them into place, and if your hand slips, it snaps back and takes a chunk out of your knuckle. I was out there in the backyard for three hours wrestling with it. The grass was still wet from the morning dew, so my knees were completely soaked by the time I finished. It's funny how dew works, just condensation from the temperature dropping overnight. It always reminds me of camping out in the backyard when I was a kid. We used to catch fireflies in a mason jar and poke holes in the lid so they could breathe...\n\nBut, uh, no. No ADHD for me. I did fine, got decent grades. I was a pretty quiet kid, mostly just kept to myself and did my work. \n\nI actually really liked getting ready for school every year, you know, buying the supplies in September.
